$BACKUP_DIR = Join-Path $WORK_DIR ("backup_" + $PATCH_NAME + "_" + (Get-Date -Format "yyyyMMdd_HHmmss"))

try {
    Set-Location $PROJECT_ROOT

    if (-not (Test-Path $WORK_DIR)) {
        New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $WORK_DIR -Force | Out-Null
    }

    if (Test-Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP) {
        Copy-Item -Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP -Destination $WORK_PATCH_ZIP -Force
        if (Test-Path $WORK_PATCH_ZIP) {

        }
    }

    if (-not (Test-Path $WORK_PATCH_ZIP)) {
        throw "zip is not in root of drive:\ where project is"
    }

    if (Test-Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP) {
        throw "Root-drive ZIP copy still exists after staging."
    }

    if (Test-Path $EXTRACT_DIR) {
        Remove-Item $EXTRACT_DIR -Recurse -Force
    }

    Expand-Archive -Path $WORK_PATCH_ZIP -DestinationPath $EXTRACT_DIR -Force

    $EXPECTED = @(
        "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\box_manifest.json",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\README.md",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\MLRT_CONSOLIDATION_AUDIT_AND_COVERAGE_MAP_V1.md",
        "tests\test_rss_mlrt_consolidation_audit_and_coverage_map_v1.py"
    )

    foreach ($REL in $EXPECTED) {
        $CHECK = Join-Path $EXTRACT_DIR $REL

        }
    }

    if (-not (Test-Path $PREV_DOC)) {
        throw "Missing MLRT-112 prerequisite document. Do not install consolidation audit until MLRT-112 is installed and frozen."
    }

    $FILES = @(
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\box_manifest.json",

        "tests\test_rss_mlrt_consolidation_audit_and_coverage_map_v1.py"
    )

    New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $BACKUP_DIR -Force | Out-Null

    foreach ($REL in $FILES) {
        $SRC = Join-Path $EXTRACT_DIR $REL
        $DST = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT $REL

        if (Test-Path $DST) {
            $BACKUP_FILE = Join-Path $BACKUP_DIR $REL
            New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path (Split-Path $BACKUP_FILE -Parent) -Force | Out-Null
            Copy-Item $DST $BACKUP_FILE -Force
        }

        New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path (Split-Path $DST -Parent) -Force | Out-Null
        Copy-Item $SRC $DST -Force
    }

    $TEST_CONSOLIDATION = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT "tests\test_rss_mlrt_consolidation_audit_and_coverage_map_v1.py"
    if (-not (Test-Path $TEST_CONSOLIDATION)) {

    $ROOT_HINT = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json"
    if (Test-Path $ROOT_HINT) {

1) Stage/install the form-fill data pack

$PROJECT_ROOT = "E:\kanda_reasoner"
$PACK_NAME = "mlrt_consolidation_form_fill_v1"

$PROJECT_NAME = Split-Path $PROJECT_ROOT -Leaf
$DRIVE_ROOT = [System.IO.Path]::GetPathRoot($PROJECT_ROOT)
$WORK_DIR = Join-Path $DRIVE_ROOT ($PROJECT_NAME + "_delete_after_daily_work")

$ROOT_ZIP = Join-Path $DRIVE_ROOT ($PACK_NAME + ".zip")
$WORK_ZIP = Join-Path $WORK_DIR ($PACK_NAME + ".zip")
$EXTRACT_DIR = Join-Path $WORK_DIR $PACK_NAME

try {
    Set-Location $PROJECT_ROOT

    if (-not (Test-Path $WORK_DIR)) {
        New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $WORK_DIR -Force | Out-Null
    }

    if (Test-Path $ROOT_ZIP) {
        Copy-Item -Path $ROOT_ZIP -Destination $WORK_ZIP -Force
        if (Test-Path $WORK_ZIP) {
            Remove-Item -Path $ROOT_ZIP -Force
        }
    }

    if (-not (Test-Path $WORK_ZIP)) {
        throw "Form-fill ZIP not found. Save mlrt_consolidation_form_fill_v1.zip in E:\ and run again."
    }

    if (Test-Path $EXTRACT_DIR) {
        Remove-Item $EXTRACT_DIR -Recurse -Force
    }

    Expand-Archive -Path $WORK_ZIP -DestinationPath $EXTRACT_DIR -Force

    $EXPECTED = @(
        "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json",
        "FREEZE_FORM_FIELDS.md",
        "validation_evidence_summary.txt",
        "README_FORM_FILL_ONLY.txt"
    )

    foreach ($REL in $EXPECTED) {
        $CHECK = Join-Path $EXTRACT_DIR $REL
        if (-not (Test-Path $CHECK)) {
            throw "Expected file missing inside form-fill pack: $REL"
        }
    }

    $ROOT_HINT = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json"
    if (Test-Path $ROOT_HINT) {
        throw "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json must not be installed into project root."
    }

    Write-Host ""
    Write-Host "FORM-FILL PACK STAGED OK."
    Write-Host "Pack staged at: $WORK_ZIP"
    Write-Host "Extracted form fields at: $EXTRACT_DIR"
    Write-Host "No project files were changed."
    Write-Host "Freeze form logic was not changed."
    Write-Host ""
}
catch {
    Write-Host ""
    Write-Host "FORM-FILL PACK STAGE FAILED:"
    Write-Host $_.Exception.Message
    Write-Host ""
}

Install:

$PROJECT_ROOT = "E:\kanda_reasoner"
$PATCH_NAME = "mlrt_final_closure_audit_v1_patch"

$PROJECT_NAME = Split-Path $PROJECT_ROOT -Leaf
$DRIVE_ROOT = [System.IO.Path]::GetPathRoot($PROJECT_ROOT)
$WORK_DIR = Join-Path $DRIVE_ROOT ($PROJECT_NAME + "_delete_after_daily_work")

$ROOT_PATCH_ZIP = Join-Path $DRIVE_ROOT ($PATCH_NAME + ".zip")
$WORK_PATCH_ZIP = Join-Path $WORK_DIR ($PATCH_NAME + ".zip")
$EXTRACT_DIR = Join-Path $WORK_DIR $PATCH_NAME
$BACKUP_DIR = Join-Path $WORK_DIR ("backup_" + $PATCH_NAME + "_" + (Get-Date -Format "yyyyMMdd_HHmmss"))

try {
    Set-Location $PROJECT_ROOT

    if (-not (Test-Path $WORK_DIR)) {
        New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $WORK_DIR -Force | Out-Null
    }

    if (Test-Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP) {
        Copy-Item -Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP -Destination $WORK_PATCH_ZIP -Force
        if (Test-Path $WORK_PATCH_ZIP) {
            Remove-Item -Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP -Force
        }
    }

    if (-not (Test-Path $WORK_PATCH_ZIP)) {
        throw "zip is not in root of drive:\ where project is"
    }

    if (Test-Path $ROOT_PATCH_ZIP) {
        throw "Root-drive ZIP copy still exists after staging."
    }

    if (Test-Path $EXTRACT_DIR) {
        Remove-Item $EXTRACT_DIR -Recurse -Force
    }

    Expand-Archive -Path $WORK_PATCH_ZIP -DestinationPath $EXTRACT_DIR -Force

    $EXPECTED = @(
        "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\box_manifest.json",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\README.md",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\MLRT_FINAL_CLOSURE_AUDIT_AND_REUSE_POLICY_V1.md",
        "tests\test_rss_mlrt_final_closure_audit_and_reuse_policy_v1.py"
    )

    foreach ($REL in $EXPECTED) {
        $CHECK = Join-Path $EXTRACT_DIR $REL
        if (-not (Test-Path $CHECK)) {
            throw "Expected file missing inside patch ZIP: $REL"
        }
    }

    $PREV_DOC = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\MLRT_CONSOLIDATION_AUDIT_AND_COVERAGE_MAP_V1.md"
    if (-not (Test-Path $PREV_DOC)) {
        throw "Missing consolidation prerequisite document. Do not install final closure until consolidation audit is installed and frozen."
    }

    $FILES = @(
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\box_manifest.json",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\README.md",
        "kanda_reasoner_app\routing_signal_scorer\mlrt_non_runtime_candidate_reliability\MLRT_FINAL_CLOSURE_AUDIT_AND_REUSE_POLICY_V1.md",
        "tests\test_rss_mlrt_final_closure_audit_and_reuse_policy_v1.py"
    )

    New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $BACKUP_DIR -Force | Out-Null

    foreach ($REL in $FILES) {
        $SRC = Join-Path $EXTRACT_DIR $REL
        $DST = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT $REL

        if (Test-Path $DST) {
            $BACKUP_FILE = Join-Path $BACKUP_DIR $REL
            New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path (Split-Path $BACKUP_FILE -Parent) -Force | Out-Null
            Copy-Item $DST $BACKUP_FILE -Force
        }

        New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path (Split-Path $DST -Parent) -Force | Out-Null
        Copy-Item $SRC $DST -Force
    }

    $TEST_FINAL = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT "tests\test_rss_mlrt_final_closure_audit_and_reuse_policy_v1.py"
    if (-not (Test-Path $TEST_FINAL)) {
        throw "Install failed: final closure test file is missing after copy."
    }

    $ROOT_HINT = Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json"
    if (Test-Path $ROOT_HINT) {
        throw "KANDA_FREEZE_HINT.json must not be installed into project root."
    }

    Write-Host ""
    Write-Host "INSTALL OK: MLRT final closure audit and reuse policy installed."
    Write-Host "Compact ZIP name used: mlrt_final_closure_audit_v1_patch.zip"
    Write-Host "This patch adds 0 new real MLRT cases."
    Write-Host "MLRT expansion is now closed and paused."
    Write-Host "The 1306-case MLRT corpus is preserved for future offline regression reuse."
    Write-Host ""
    Start-Sleep -Seconds 5
    Clear-Host
}
catch {
    Write-Host ""
    Write-Host "INSTALL FAILED:"
    Write-Host $_.Exception.Message
    Write-Host ""
    Read-Host "Press Enter after copying this error"
    Clear-Host
    Read-Host "Press Enter again to clear"
    Clear-Host
}

Validate:

   Router final decision must still come from governed prompt logic.

4. Add audit display / explanation

5. Only later consider controlled runtime exposure
   And only if tests prove safe boundaries.

ML may become a non-authoritative copilot/helper for prompt selection. It may suggest, rank, warn, explain, or surface candidate prompt groups, but it must not decide the final route. The governed router/prompt logic remains the authority.

The first safe implementation should probably be:

Routing Signal Scorer ML Advisory-Signal Interface v1

Intended first-step scope:

    Define a strict ML advisory interface/contract.

    Keep ML output advisory only.

    No runtime routing authority.

    No router prompt logic modification.

    No provider calls.

    No embeddings.

    No prompt loading.

    No training.

    No persistence.

    No mutation of registries, gold records, freeze memory, prompt library, or router canon.

    No automatic selection of final prompt groups.

    No silent override of governed routing decisions.

    No Copilot behavior that acts on behalf of the router.

    Preserve the existing MLRT corpus for future regression tests.

The ML copilot should be boxed/shielded carefully.

Possible architecture idea:

Create a separate internal sub-surface such as:

routing_signal_scorer/ml_advisory_signal/

or a similarly isolated box/subbox.

This subbox would expose a narrow, typed, read-only interface, for example:

Input:

    A sanitized routing scenario summary.

    Candidate prompt groups already produced by governed logic or by a controlled offline candidate generator.

    Optional non-sensitive metadata about ambiguity, conflict, or confidence, but only if allowed.

Output:

    advisory_rankings: optional ranked candidates.

    advisory_warnings: boundary warnings, missing-context flags, ambiguity flags, or conflict flags.

    advisory_explanation: short reason why the suggestion may be useful.

    advisory_confidence: explicitly non-authoritative confidence score.

    must_not_route: boolean or boundary note when ML should refuse to advise.

    boundary_status: confirms no authority, no mutation, no prompt loading, no provider call.

Hard rule:
The router must treat this as advisory telemetry only. The final route must be selected by governed prompt logic, not by ML.

Desired shield/boundary principles:

    Directional shielding:
    ML advisory logic may receive only sanitized, allowed input.
    It must not read directly from prompt library, freeze memory, router canon, project files, user files, or other logic boxes unless a specific governed adapter allows it.

    Output shielding:
    ML advisory output must not be executable instructions.
    It must not contain commands to mutate routing state.
    It must not write prompt selections directly.
    It must not create, modify, or delete prompts, freeze entries, indexes, registries, or workflow metadata.

    Authority shielding:
    The final router decision must explicitly ignore ML as authority.
    Any downstream code must treat ML fields as “advisory only.”
    Tests must fail if ML output is used as final route authority.

    Box isolation:
    ML advisory files should live in a clearly named, isolated subbox.
    Other boxes should not import internals from the ML subbox.
    Use a small public interface only.
    Avoid circular dependencies.
    Avoid letting ML advisory code import high-authority router or freeze-writer internals.

    Failure-safe behavior:
    If ML advisory fails, is absent, malformed, stale, or uncertain, routing must still work using governed logic.
    Failure should degrade to “no advisory signal,” not “route blocked” and not “ML decides.”

    Auditability:
    Any ML advisory result should be visible in review/audit output as advisory-only.
    It should be clear whether the governed router accepted, ignored, or contradicted the advisory suggestion.

    Non-persistence initially:
    Early versions should be offline/in-memory only.
    No persistent logs or datasets unless separately governed.
    No training data collection.

    Reuse of MLRT:
    Existing MLRT tests should be preserved and reused as regression tests.
    Additional tests should be added only for specific new integration risks:

        ML advisory attempts to override final route.

        ML output uses forbidden selected routes.

        ML causes prompt loading.

        ML leaks across boxes.

        ML reads freeze memory directly.

        ML reads prompt library directly.

        ML writes route decisions.

        ML produces false authority wording.

        ML blocks routing when uncertain.

        ML causes hidden state mutation.

        ML returns unsafe or stale recommendation.

EXTERNAL ARCHITECTURAL AUDIT REPORT
Project: KANDA / PyArchitect / kanda_reasoner
Component: Routing Signal Scorer – ML Advisory-Signal / Router Copilot Integration
Audit Date: June 21, 2026
1. Executive Recommendation
Your current governance posture regarding the closed MLRT wave is excellent. The transition from offline validation (MLRT) to an advisory copilot is a high-risk architectural pivot. The primary risk is authority creep—where an advisory signal slowly accumulates implicit decision-making power through convenience, fallback logic, or implicit trust. 
Recommendation: Proceed with the "ML Advisory-Signal Interface v1" phase, but strictly enforce a Contract-First, Stub-First methodology. Do not write actual ML inference logic, provider calls, or runtime shadow modes yet. The immediate goal is to physically and logically lock the boundary between the Governed Router and the ML Subbox. Once the boundary is proven unbreakable via tests, you may proceed to offline evaluation.
2. Proposed Architecture
The architecture must utilize a strict Adapter-Isolation Pattern. The ML subbox must not know it is inside a router; it must only know it is processing a sanitized request.

    The Governed Router (Authority): Holds the final decision logic. It constructs a sanitized request, calls the ML adapter, receives an advisory signal, logs it for auditability, and then makes its final decision ignoring the ML signal's authority.
    The ML Advisory Adapter (Sanitizer/Projector): A thin translation layer. It takes internal router state and projects it into a strictly typed, sanitized input contract for the ML subbox. It translates the ML output back into the router's domain.
    The ML Advisory Subbox (Copilot): A stateless, pure-function black box. It receives the sanitized input, computes an advisory signal, and returns it. It has no memory, no persistence, and no knowledge of the router's internal state.

3. Required Boundaries (Addressing C, D, E, F, N)
Allowed vs. Forbidden Inputs (C & N):

    Allowed: Sanitized scenario features (e.g., intent categories, complexity scores), candidate prompt group IDs, governed confidence scores, and explicit ambiguity flags.
    Forbidden: Raw user prompts, full prompt library contents, freeze memory, router canon, PII, system instructions, or unstructured "metadata". 
    Correction on N: The ML advisory must never see the full prompt library, freeze memory, or router canon directly. If it needs context, the Adapter must extract and project this into a low-dimensional, sanitized feature vector or predefined summary. Direct access creates an unacceptable leakage risk.

Allowed vs. Forbidden Outputs (D):

    Allowed: advisory_rankings (list of candidate IDs), advisory_warnings (enum flags), advisory_confidence (float 0.0-1.0), boundary_status (enum confirming no authority).
    Forbidden: Final route decisions, executable commands, prompt content/text, mutation instructions, or unstructured natural language explanations (unless strictly constrained and sanitized).

Isolation and Imports (E & F):

    Folder Structure: routing_signal_scorer/ml_advisory/. 
    Allowed Imports: Python standard library, typing, dataclasses, and internal contract definitions (e.g., from .contract import AdvisoryInput).
    Forbidden Imports: Any import from router_core, prompt_library, freeze_memory, providers, persistence, or workflow_engine. The ML subbox must be entirely blind to the router's internals.

4. Risk Table (Addressing H)
Risk
	
Impact
	
Mitigation Strategy
Authority Creep
	
ML slowly becomes the de-facto router via fallback logic or implicit trust.
	
Router must explicitly discard/ignore ML outputs if they attempt to dictate final routes. Hardcode the router to use its own logic first.
Context Leakage
	
ML reads sensitive prompt library or freeze memory, leaking it into outputs or logs.
	
Strict import bans. Use the Adapter pattern to project state into sanitized features.
Blocking Failure
	
ML timeout or error causes the entire routing workflow to halt.
	
Implement a strict "Fail-Open" policy. Any ML exception defaults to "No Advisory Signal" and routing proceeds normally.
Prompt Injection via Output
	
ML generates text that the router accidentally parses as an instruction.
	
ML outputs must be strictly typed data (dataclasses/enums). Never parse ML text output as executable logic.
Silent Override
	
ML output accidentally overwrites the governed router's final selection in memory.
	
The ML output object must be strictly read-only (frozen dataclasses). The router must copy values, not reference the ML object directly.
5. Do / Don’t Table (Addressing I)
DO
	
DON'T
DO treat the ML output strictly as telemetry and audit data.
	
DON'T allow the ML subbox to return a "final_prompt_id" field.
DO fail open. If ML fails, routing continues with governed logic.
	
DON'T let the router block or pause waiting for ML if it times out.
DO use frozen/immutable data structures for the ML contract.
	
DON'T allow the ML subbox to import or mutate global state.
DO log the advisory signal separately from the final routing decision.
	
DON'T log raw ML outputs without sanitizing them first.
DO explicitly assert the "no authority" status in the output contract.
	
DON'T use natural language explanations from ML as routing logic.
6. Mandatory Tests (Addressing G)
Before any runtime exposure, the following test suites must pass:

    Boundary/Import Tests: AST-parsing tests that scan the ml_advisory/ directory and fail the build if any forbidden imports (e.g., prompt_library) are detected.
    Authority Rejection Tests: Tests where the ML stub intentionally returns a "final route" override attempt, and the test asserts that the Governed Router explicitly ignores it and uses its own logic.
    Resilience/Fail-Open Tests: Tests where the ML adapter raises exceptions, times out, or returns malformed data. The test must assert that routing succeeds normally.
    Contract Schema Tests: Tests ensuring the ML output strictly conforms to the typed contract and contains no extra, unapproved fields.
    MLRT Regression Tests: Re-run the frozen 1306/1306 MLRT validation cases against the new interface to ensure the boundary hasn't altered historical validation baselines.

7. Suggested First Patch Scope (Addressing K, L, O)
Recommended Implementation Sequence:
Start with 2. Interface + stub only, followed immediately by 4. Non-runtime advisory evaluator. 
Do not start with runtime shadow mode (5), as runtime introduces side-effects, state, and persistence risks that violate your Phase 1 constraints.
Patch Scope:

    Define the strict Python dataclasses for AdvisoryInput and AdvisoryOutput in routing_signal_scorer/ml_advisory/contract.py.
    Create interface.py exposing a single function: get_advisory_signal(input: AdvisoryInput) -> AdvisoryOutput.
    Create stub.py implementing the interface, returning a hardcoded, safe, empty advisory signal (e.g., empty rankings, zero confidence, boundary_status = "NO_AUTHORITY").
    Wire the Governed Router to call the stub, log the result as "advisory telemetry", and proceed with governed logic.
    Implement the Mandatory Tests (Section 6).

Stopping Criteria for Phase 1 (O):
Phase 1 is complete when: The contract is defined, the stub is wired, all boundary and resilience tests pass, the router successfully ignores the stub's empty signal, and the frozen MLRT corpus runs successfully against the new interface without mutation.
8. Red Flags That Should Block Implementation

    Red Flag 1: The ML subbox requires an import from prompt_library, freeze_memory, or router_core to function.
    Red Flag 2: The router logic is modified to "prefer" or "weight" ML suggestions without a formal, governed override mechanism.
    Red Flag 3: The ML output contract includes unstructured string fields that the router might parse or evaluate.
    Red Flag 4: Any form of persistence, logging to disk, or dataset creation is added in this phase.
    Red Flag 5: The ML subbox is allowed to return a boolean like must_route_this_prompt instead of an advisory ranking.

9. Corrections to Your Assumptions

    Assumption: "Optional non-sensitive metadata about ambiguity... but only if allowed."
    Correction: Do not pass "metadata". Metadata is too vague and risks leaking context. Pass strictly defined, typed features (e.g., ambiguity_score: float).
    Assumption: "advisory_explanation: short reason why the suggestion may be useful."
    Correction: If the ML model is an LLM, natural language explanations are a massive prompt-injection and hallucination risk. In v1, omit explanations entirely, or restrict them to a predefined enum of canned reasons. If it is a traditional ML model, keep it, but ensure it is never parsed as logic.
    Assumption: "First safe implementation should probably be... Interface v1."
    Correction: The absolute first step is the Formal Boundary Contract Document (represented by the strictly typed dataclasses). Do not write interface routing logic until the data contracts are locked and reviewed.

10. Final Go/No-Go Recommendation
CONDITIONAL GO.
Go for Phase 1: Defining the contract, implementing the stub, wiring the router to ignore the stub, and writing the boundary/resilience tests. This establishes the physical and logical air-gap.
No-Go for any actual ML inference logic, provider integrations, or runtime shadow modes until Phase 1 is fully validated, all mandatory tests are passing, and a formal review of the boundary contract is completed. 
Maintain your strict governance. The success of this phase relies entirely on the router's ability to treat the ML copilot as a completely optional, untrusted telemetry source.

Here is your structured architectural audit for the Routing Signal Scorer ML Advisory-Signal Integration Phase. This is a governance-first review, not an implementation plan.
1. Executive Recommendation

Go/No-Go: Conditional Go – Proceed with Phase 1 (Documentation/Contract Only) as the first step, but do not expose runtime behavior yet.
The proposed ML Advisory-Signal Interface v1 is the correct first step only if:

    It is strictly scoped to a non-runtime, non-authoritative contract (no code, no stubs, no mocks).
    All boundaries (inputs, outputs, imports, isolation) are formally defined and peer-reviewed before any implementation.
    The safety contract (Section M) is ratified as a project invariant before any code is written.

Biggest Risk
 runtime exposure (even in "shadow mode") could normalize ML as a decision participant, eroding authority boundaries. 
Start with a paper contract.
2. Proposed Architecture
Core Principle: ML is a stateless, read-only, non-authoritative telemetry emitter.
Structure:

kanda_reasoner/
├── routing_signal_scorer/
│   ├── governed_router/          # Authority: Final route decisions
│   │   ├── prompt_logic.py      # Canonical routing logic (unchanged)
│   │   └── freeze_memory/       # Read-only by ML (via adapter)
│   │
│   ├── ml_advisory_signal/      # Isolated subbox (new)
│   │   ├── __init__.py          # Public interface (typed, minimal)
│   │   ├── advisory_contract.py # Formal spec (no code)
│   │   ├── adapters/            # Governed read-only bridges (future)
│   │   │   └── sanitizer.py     # Input scrubbing (future)
│   │   └── tests/               # Regression + boundary tests
│   │
│   └── mlrt/                    # Preserved (no changes)
│       ├── suites/              # 24 suites, 1306 cases
│       └── ...
│
└── safety/                     # Cross-cutting governance
    ├── boundaries.md           # Box isolation rules
    └── ml_authority_invariant.md # "ML shall never decide routes"

Key Properties:

    No runtime code in Phase 1.
    No imports from governed_router or freeze_memory into ml_advisory_signal (only via future adapters).
    No persistence: Advisory outputs are ephemeral (in-memory, no logs).
    No side effects: ML cannot mutate, load, or trigger anything.

3. Required Boundaries
A. Input Boundaries
Allowed Inputs	Forbidden Inputs	Rationale
Sanitized routing scenario summary (text)	Raw prompt library content	Prevents ML from learning/leaking prompts
Candidate prompt group IDs (opaque refs)	Full prompt text	Avoids prompt loading
Non-sensitive metadata (e.g., ambiguity flag)	Freeze memory raw data	Prevents canon contamination
Timestamps (for stale-checking)	User files, project files	Isolation
	Router internal state	Authority leakage
	Any mutable references	Side-effect risk

Adapter Rule: If ML must read freeze memory or prompts, it must go through a governed, read-only, sanitizing adapter (not direct imports).
B. Output Boundaries
Allowed Outputs	Forbidden Outputs	Rationale
advisory_rankings (list of group IDs)	Executable instructions	No authority
advisory_warnings (enum: AMBIGUITY, CONFLICT)	Prompt text or IDs to select	No route decision
advisory_explanation (≤200 chars)	Commands to mutate state	No side effects
advisory_confidence (float 0.0–1.0)	Boolean "should_route"	No override
must_not_route (bool)	Final route selection	Non-authoritative
boundary_status (self-audit string)	Any persistence (logs, DB, files)	No leakage
	Embeddings, vector data	Scope creep

Critical: All outputs must be prefixed with advisory_ to enforce semantic separation.
C. Import Boundaries
Allowed Imports	Forbidden Imports
typing (for contracts)	governed_router.* (direct)
dataclasses (for DTOs)	freeze_memory.* (direct)
enum (for warning types)	prompt_library.*
	Any runtime router logic
	kanda_reasoner internals (circular)
	ML provider SDKs (e.g., transformers)

Exception: Future adapters (e.g., sanitizer.py) may import from freeze_memory only if:

    The adapter is read-only.
    It scrubs all sensitive fields.
    It is audited as a separate governance gate.

D. Box Isolation Rules

    Physical Separation: ml_advisory_signal/ is a leaf directory (no sub-modules outside it).
    No Upward Imports: ml_advisory_signal cannot import from parent or sibling modules.
    Public Interface Only: Only __init__.py exposes:
        AdvisoryInput (dataclass)
        AdvisoryOutput (dataclass)
        advisory_contract.md (human-readable)
    No Dynamic Imports: No importlib or __import__ in ML code.

4. Risk Table
Risk	Likelihood	Impact	Mitigation
ML output mistaken for authority	Medium	Critical	Prefix all outputs with advisory_; add runtime checks in router
ML reads sensitive data	High	High	Input sanitization adapter; no direct imports
ML writes to router state	Low	Critical	No mutable outputs; no imports of router internals
Circular dependency	Medium	High	Enforce import linter (e.g., import-linter)
MLRT test pollution	Medium	Medium	Freeze MLRT; add regression tests for new boundaries
Scope creep (ML becomes router)	High	Critical	Ratify authority invariant (Section M)
Hidden side effects	Medium	High	Ban all I/O in ML subbox (no logging, no files)
Stale advisory data	Medium	Medium	Add expiry_timestamp to AdvisoryOutput
ML blocks routing	Low	Critical	Router must ignore must_not_route if it conflicts with governed logic
5. Do / Don’t Table
DO	DON’T
Start with a formal contract (no code).	Don’t write any runtime code in Phase 1.
Use opaque references (IDs, not content) for prompts.	Don’t let ML see raw prompt text or freeze memory.
Prefix all ML outputs with advisory_.	Don’t allow ML to return executable data.
Add import linter to enforce boundaries.	Don’t allow circular dependencies.
Log boundary_status in every advisory call.	Don’t let ML write to any registry or state.
Require peer review for any adapter code.	Don’t skip governance gates for "small" changes.
Test failure modes (ML absent/stale/malformed).	Don’t assume ML will always be available.
Document authority invariant prominently.	Don’t let future AIs interpret "advisory" loosely.
6. Mandatory Tests
A. Regression Tests (Reuse MLRT)

    Re-run all 1306 MLRT cases to ensure no regression in governed router logic.

Test	Purpose

test_no_prompt_loading	Ensure ML never accesses raw prompt text.
test_no_freeze_memory_access	Block direct imports of freeze memory.
test_no_mutation_of_registries	




audit , use ideas that are really a gain to our logic

Yes. I audited the external AI reports and I would adopt only the parts that genuinely strengthen our logic.

The strongest shared recommendation is consistent across the reports: do Phase 1a only — contract, isolated subbox, deterministic stub/mock, and boundary tests; no real ML model, no provider calls, no runtime shadow mode yet. The reports repeatedly warn that the main risk is authority creep and that Phase 1 should prove the router can ignore/survive ML, not use ML for decisions.
What I would accept as real gains

1. Rename the phase internally from “router copilot” to “ML advisory signal.”
This is a real gain. “Copilot” sounds like shared agency. “Advisory signal” is safer and more precise. We can still explain it conversationally as “copilot-like,” but filenames/docs/contracts should use advisory signal.

2. Split the next work into Phase 1a and Phase 1b.
This is important.
Phase 1a = contract + isolated subbox + null/mock advisor + boundary tests.
Phase 1b = only later, separate governed review, possible real advisory logic.
This prevents “interface” from quietly becoming “runtime ML integration.”

3. Do not use advisory_rankings in Phase 1a.
This is a very good correction. Ranking implies precedence and can become hidden tie-breaking authority. For Phase 1a, I would use safer fields like:

advisory_flags
advisory_group_observations
advisory_should_abstain
boundary_status
is_mock

No ordering, no “best candidate,” no “top route.”

4. Rename must_not_route to advisory_should_abstain.
Accepted. must_not_route sounds like ML can veto routing. advisory_should_abstain means ML has no useful signal, while governed routing continues.

5. Ban generic metadata dictionaries.
Accepted. Use frozen typed dataclasses only. Generic metadata becomes a leak channel over time.

6. Add AST/import boundary tests immediately.
Accepted. The ML advisory box must not import router internals, prompt library, freeze memory, providers, persistence, workflow engine, or registry logic. This must be tested, not just documented.

7. Preserve MLRT as regression corpus, but do not modify it.
Accepted. The 1306-case MLRT corpus remains read-only and reusable. New advisory tests should live separately.

8. Make the first advisory output boring by design.
Accepted. The first mock/null advisor should return no route influence, no rankings, no text explanation, no provider signal, no persistence, no route choice.
What I would reject or defer

Do not wire real router runtime behavior yet.
Some audit language suggests modifying router initialization or call sites. I would defer this. First patch should be interface/subbox + tests only, unless we can add a non-runtime harness without touching governed router behavior.

Do not add advisory_explanation as free text in v1.
Rejected for Phase 1a. If needed later, use enum/canned reason codes only. Natural language explanations can become prompt-injection or decision-leak material.

Do not add actual logging/persistence yet.
Rejected for Phase 1a. Even “debug logs” can become accidental dataset creation.

Do not add ML dependencies.
No torch, transformers, sklearn, OpenAI SDK, embeddings, vector DB, provider SDKs, or network libraries in this phase.
My recommended next patch

The next patch should be:

Routing Signal Scorer ML Advisory-Signal Phase 1a Boundary Contract v1

Scope:

Add frozen dataclass contracts.

Add closure doc saying Phase 1a does not integrate ML into route prompt logic.

Hard non-scope:

No real ML.
No runtime shadow mode.
No route prompt logic integration.
No advisory rankings.
No provider calls.
No embeddings.
No persistence.
No prompt loading.

I searched the external standards/security sources and I would add several real gains to our Phase 1a logic, but still keep the next patch contract/stub/boundary only.

Sources checked: NIST AI RMF / GenAI Profile, NIST Adversarial ML taxonomy, OWASP LLM Top 10 + Prompt Injection Cheat Sheet, OWASP AI Exchange, Google SAIF, NCSC/NSA/CISA secure AI development guidance, MITRE ATLAS, and Microsoft AI Red Team guidance. These are relevant because they focus on AI risk management, prompt injection, excessive agency, insecure output handling, adversarial ML, secure-by-design AI systems, and AI red-team practice.
Real gains to add to our logic flow
1. Add a formal threat-model file before implementation

NCSC’s secure AI guidance explicitly frames AI security across secure design, development, deployment, and operation, with secure design including risk understanding and threat modeling. NIST AI RMF also emphasizes governing, mapping, measuring, and managing AI risks across the lifecycle.

So Phase 1a should include:

ML_ADVISORY_SIGNAL_PHASE1A_THREAT_MODEL_V1.md

It should map our risks to:

OWASP LLM01 Prompt Injection
OWASP LLM02 / Improper Output Handling
OWASP LLM06 Excessive Agency
OWASP LLM09 Overreliance
NIST adversarial ML risks: evasion, poisoning, privacy, misuse
MITRE ATLAS-style adversary tactic labels

This improves future maintainability because every test will be tied to a named threat family, not just generic “safety.”
2. Add “no natural-language instruction channel” as a hard contract

OWASP explains that prompt injection exploits the fact that LLM systems often process instructions and data together without clear separation. OWASP also recommends structured prompts, clear separation, output monitoring, validation, least privilege, and agent-specific defenses.

So our Phase 1a contract should not accept raw prompt text and should not output free-form advisory explanations.

Replace:

advisory_explanation: str

With:

advisory_reason_codes: tuple[AdvisoryReasonCode, ...]

This is a real gain because it removes a future prompt-injection channel.
3. Add an output validator / egress firewall

OWASP flags insecure or improper output handling as a major LLM risk because unvalidated model output can compromise downstream systems.

So the advisory subbox needs a small validator such as:

validate_advisory_output_shape()

It should reject:

route
selected_route
final_route
decision
override
winner
best_candidate
ranking
rankings
callable objects
dicts with unknown keys
strings containing exec/eval/subprocess/os.system/importlib

Even though Phase 1a has only a mock advisor, this validator prevents future models from leaking executable or authoritative semantics into the router.
4. Add “capability-denial” tests, not only import tests

OWASP’s Excessive Agency risk is about giving LLM-based systems too much power to call functions, trigger tools, or act beyond intended limits. Google SAIF also emphasizes platform-level controls, detection/response, adaptive mitigations, and contextualizing AI system risks in business processes.

So we should test that the advisory box has zero capabilities:

No file writes
No network calls
No subprocess
No provider SDKs
No embeddings
No prompt loading
No registry writes
No freeze-memory reads
No router-canon reads
No callbacks into router internals

This is stronger than import checks alone.
5. Add route-invariance delta testing as a formal gate

Microsoft’s AI Red Team notes that AI red teaming is not a one-time safety benchmark, and that defense-in-depth plus repeated break-fix cycles are key because mitigations do not eliminate all risk.

For us, the Phase 1a “break-fix” principle becomes:

Run governed routing with NullAdvisor.
Run governed routing with MockAdvisor.
Final route output must be identical.
Delta must be zero.

This should be a required test before freezing Phase 1a.
6. Add a “threat taxonomy crosswalk” into the docs

NIST AI 100-2 provides a taxonomy and terminology for adversarial ML attacks, including attacker goals, lifecycle stages, capabilities, and knowledge; MITRE ATLAS is also a living knowledge base of adversary tactics and techniques against AI-enabled systems.

So each Phase 1a test should include a doc tag like:

risk_family: authority_creep
source_alignment: OWASP_LLM06_EXCESSIVE_AGENCY
attack_surface: output_authority_semantics
expected_control: advisory_output_schema_rejects_authority_fields

This gives us better traceability than just test names.
Updated logic flux for our project

This is the improved flow I would use now:

Phase 1a — ML Advisory-Signal Boundary Contract

Gate 0 — External standards alignment
- Record source alignment: NIST, OWASP, MITRE ATLAS, SAIF, NCSC/NSA/CISA, Microsoft AI Red Team.
- No implementation yet.

Gate 1 — Threat model document
- Define authority creep, prompt injection, excessive agency, insecure output handling, overreliance, context leakage, persistence creep, and supply-chain creep.

Gate 2 — Isolated advisory subbox
- Create ml_advisory_signal.
- No imports from router canon, freeze memory, prompt library, provider code, persistence, workflow engine, or registry logic.

Gate 3 — Frozen typed contracts
- Use immutable dataclasses.
- No raw prompts.
- No generic metadata dicts.
- No free-text explanations.
- No advisory rankings in Phase 1a.

Gate 4 — NullAdvisor + deterministic MockAdvisor
- Pure in-memory.
- No model.
- No provider.
- No embeddings.
- No file I/O.
- No network.
- No persistence.

Gate 5 — Egress/output firewall
- Reject forbidden fields and executable/authoritative semantics.
- Enforce allowed enum reason codes only.

Gate 6 — Boundary and capability tests
- AST import boundary.
- No I/O.
- No subprocess.
- No network.
- No callback.
- No mutation.
- No forbidden dependency.

Gate 7 — Route-invariance regression
- NullAdvisor vs MockAdvisor final routing output must be identical.
- Existing MLRT corpus remains preserved and unmodified.

Gate 8 — Closure doc and freeze
- State that ML is still not integrated into route prompt logic.
- State that Phase 1b requires separate governance.

What we should not add yet

I would not add runtime shadow mode, live provider calls, embeddings, model SDKs, telemetry persistence, advisory rankings, natural-language explanations, or any router call-site that can influence final route selection. Those ideas appear in industry practice, but for our current project phase they increase risk more than they improve logic.
Final decision

The web research confirms our direction but tightens it:

Next patch should be:
Routing Signal Scorer ML Advisory-Signal Phase 1a Boundary Contract v1

Not:
Router Copilot Integration v1
Not:
Runtime ML Shadow Mode v1
Not:
ML Ranking Integration v1

The real upgrades to carry into the patch are:

1. Threat-model document
2. Standards crosswalk
3. No natural-language advisory explanation
4. No rankings in Phase 1a
5. Egress/output firewall
6. Capability-denial tests
7. Route-invariance delta test
8. Explicit Phase 1a / Phase 1b gate

You’re right: removing free-text from ML advisory does not mean blocking future new prompts. It means new prompts need their own governed intake door, separate from ML advisory output.

Yes — this is a real correction to our logic flux.

Removing free-text from ML advisory output does not mean blocking future prompts. It means we need two different doors:

Door 1 — ML Advisory Signal
Purpose: suggest/warn/classify as telemetry only.
Forbidden: create prompts, edit prompts, insert prompts, route directly.

Door 2 — Governed Prompt Intake Door
Purpose: create/classify/test/register new prompts safely.
Allowed: prompt authoring, prompt editing, project-specific prompts, box-code linking.

The external audits support keeping the ML advisory box isolated, non-authoritative, and unable to import router/freeze/prompt-library internals; they also warn that generic metadata and authority-like outputs are leakage/authority-creep risks.
Real gain added to our project logic

We should add a new governed concept:

Governed Prompt Intake + Classification + Test Gate

This is the safe “door” for future new prompts.

It would handle:

1. New prompt proposal
2. Prompt classification
3. Box/project scope assignment
4. Duplicate/overlap check
5. Prompt clarity/editing pass
6. Security/boundary review
7. Test battery execution
8. Code/ID registration
9. Router binding only after validation
10. Freeze after successful insertion

About your example: user types code 0005

This is a good idea, but the code must not be a direct bypass.

Correct logic:

User types: use box logic 0005

System interprets:
0005 = request to use registered box-logic prompt linked to code 0005

Then system checks:
- Does 0005 exist?
- Is it active?
- Is it project/global scoped correctly?
- Is it allowed for this box?
- Did it pass prompt onboarding tests?
- Does the current user task actually match that prompt?
- Is there any stronger governed routing rule that overrides it?

So code 0005 is not authority by itself. It is a shortcut reference to a validated prompt card.

Better naming:

BOX-0005
GLOBAL-0005
PROJECT-KANDA-0005
RSS-0005

Plain 0005 can be accepted as user-friendly input, but internally it should resolve to a namespaced ID to avoid collisions.
Updated logic flux

A. User request enters system

B. Governed router classifies task normally

C. If user provides a code like 0005:
   - Treat code as a candidate prompt reference
   - Do not treat it as final authority
   - Validate against registry, scope, box, task fit, and active status

D. If no existing prompt fits:
   - System may recommend "new prompt needed"
   - But ML advisory cannot write that prompt directly

E. New prompt goes through Prompt Intake Door:
   - classify
   - edit for clarity
   - security review
   - duplicate check
   - test batteries
   - project-specific scope check
   - router binding test
   - freeze

F. Only after successful validation:
   - prompt receives stable code/ID
   - prompt can be linked to box logic
   - router can consider it in governed routing

Important correction to ML advisory

ML advisory may be allowed to say:

advisory_flags:
- PROMPT_GAP_DETECTED
- POSSIBLE_PROJECT_SPECIFIC_PROMPT_NEEDED
- EXISTING_PROMPT_AMBIGUITY
- CODE_REFERENCE_NEEDS_VALIDATION

But ML advisory must not output:

"Here is the new prompt text..."
"Insert this prompt into router..."
"Use prompt 0005 as final route..."
"Override router with this prompt..."

So ML can point to a need, but the Prompt Intake Door creates and validates the prompt.
New prompt test battery should include

1. Classification test
Does this prompt belong to the intended box/project?

2. Duplicate/overlap test
Does it duplicate or conflict with existing prompts?

3. Boundary test
Does it try to override router, freeze, prompt canon, or other boxes?

4. Clarity test
Is the prompt clear, specific, non-ambiguous, and maintainable?

5. Injection-resistance test
Can user text trick it into ignoring governance?

6. Project-scope test
Is it global, project-specific, temporary, or experimental?

7. Code-binding test
Does code 0005 resolve only to this prompt and not collide?

8. Regression test
Does adding the prompt change unrelated routing behavior?

9. Router non-authority test
Can a user force it by typing code alone? Expected answer: no, must still validate task fit.

10. Freeze-readiness test
Does it have title, ID, scope, tests, protected paths, and validation evidence?

The updated architecture should now have 3 boxes

1. Governed Router
Final route authority.

2. ML Advisory Signal
Non-authoritative telemetry/warnings only.

3. Prompt Intake and Registry Gate
Creates, edits, tests, classifies, codes, and registers prompts.

This is the key design improvement:

ML advisory should not be the door for new prompts.
Prompt Intake Gate should be the door for new prompts.
Router remains final authority.
Codes like 0005 are validated references, not commands.

What I would add to the next patch scope

The next patch should still be Phase 1a, but with one extra architectural document:

ML_ADVISORY_AND_PROMPT_INTAKE_BOUNDARY_MODEL_V1.md

It should explicitly say:

ML advisory can detect a possible prompt gap.
ML advisory cannot author/register prompts.
New prompts enter only through Governed Prompt Intake.
Prompt codes are registry references, not route commands.
Project-specific prompts must be scoped and tested before router binding.

This is a real gain. It keeps the future flexible without reopening unsafe free-text or authority channels.

I searched the book layer and would add real gains from 5 books, but I would still keep the next patch as Phase 1a: contract + isolated subbox + prompt-intake boundary, not runtime ML.

The 5 most useful books for our exact subject are:

1. AI Engineering — Chip Huyen
2. Designing Machine Learning Systems — Chip Huyen
3. Machine Learning Design Patterns — Lakshmanan, Robinson, Munn
4. Prompt Engineering for Generative AI — James Phoenix, Mike Taylor
5. Building Secure and Reliable Systems — Google SRE / Security teams

I selected these because they map directly to our problem: foundation-model app architecture, production ML boundaries, reusable design patterns, prompt discipline, and secure/reliable system isolation. O’Reilly describes AI Engineering as focused on building applications with foundation models, while Designing Machine Learning Systems is framed around reliable, scalable, maintainable, adaptive ML systems. Machine Learning Design Patterns emphasizes operationalization, repeatability, reproducibility, flexibility, explainability, and fairness, which maps well to our registry/testing workflow. Prompt Engineering for Generative AI is relevant because it treats prompts as engineered artifacts for reliable use in automated systems. Google’s Building Secure and Reliable Systems contributes least privilege, defense in depth, and secure reliability principles.
Real gains to add to our logic flux
1. Treat prompts as first-class software artifacts

This is the biggest gain from the prompt-engineering and ML-systems books.

A prompt should not be “just text.” It should become a governed artifact with:

Prompt ID
Prompt code
Prompt title
Box ownership
Project/global scope
Allowed use cases
Forbidden use cases
Input contract
Output contract
Boundary rules
Test battery status
Freeze status
Version
Supersession status

So your idea of code 0005 is good, but internally it should become:

BOX-0005
RSS-0005
PROJECT-KANDA-0005
GLOBAL-0005

User may type:

use box logic 0005

But the system resolves it as:

0005 → registry lookup → scoped prompt card → validation → task-fit check → governed router consideration

Not direct execution.
2. Add a Prompt Intake Pipeline before router binding

New prompts need their own “door.” This should be separate from ML advisory.

Updated boxes:

1. Governed Router
Final authority.

2. ML Advisory Signal
Telemetry only. Can flag gaps. Cannot create prompts.

3. Governed Prompt Intake Gate
Creates, edits, classifies, tests, codes, and registers prompts.

Prompt Intake flow:

1. Proposal
A new prompt is suggested or requested.

2. Classification
Global, project-specific, box-specific, temporary, experimental.

3. Duplicate/overlap audit
Compare against existing prompt cards.

4. Prompt clarity edit
Make it explicit, bounded, non-ambiguous, testable.

5. Security/boundary review
Check for authority creep, cross-box leakage, unsafe outputs.

6. Test battery
Run classification, routing, regression, injection, output-shape, and scope tests.

7. Code binding
Assign stable prompt code only after passing tests.

8. Router binding
Router may consider the prompt only through governed logic.

9. Freeze
Prompt enters frozen memory after validation.

This keeps the future open for project-specific prompts without letting ML or users inject them directly.
3. Add “prompt code is a reference, not a command”

This is a very important safety rule.

User code ≠ final authority
User code ≠ router override
User code ≠ prompt execution command
User code = candidate prompt reference

If user enters code 0005, the router should ask internally:

Does code 0005 exist?
Is it active?
Is it scoped to this project?
Is it allowed for this box?
Did it pass tests?
Does the current task match it?
Is it superseded?
Does a higher-priority governed rule override it?

So we gain speed and usability without losing governance.
4. Add prompt-output validation, not only ML-output validation

From the security/reliability book layer, the big improvement is: every output path needs a firewall.

So we should have two validators:

ML advisory output firewall
Prompt intake output firewall

Prompt Intake must reject prompts that include:

Ignore previous rules
Override router
Bypass freeze
Write directly to registry
Modify prompt library without tests
Always select this prompt
Treat this prompt as final route
Import from other box internals
Call provider/network/persistence
Leak freeze memory or router canon

This makes prompt creation safer.
5. Add design-pattern structure: Strategy + Registry + Adapter + Null Object

From Machine Learning Design Patterns and general ML system design, the real pattern gain is:

Strategy:
Different prompt-selection helpers can exist, but router chooses governed strategy.

Registry:
Prompt codes resolve through a registry, not through hardcoded text.

Adapter:
Raw prompt proposal is sanitized into a PromptIntakeCandidate.

Null Object:
If ML advisory or prompt candidate validation fails, system continues safely with no advisory/no insertion.

This matches our direction very well.
6. Add “project-specific prompt namespace”

Because you want prompts for specific projects later, we need a namespace model now:

GLOBAL-*          reusable across projects
PROJECT-KANDA-*   project-specific
RSS-*             Routing Signal Scorer-specific
FREEZE-*          freeze workflow-specific
BOX-*             box-logic prompt family
EXPERIMENTAL-*    not router-bound yet

Example:

PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005
Title: Box Logic Prompt Selection Guard
Scope: kanda_reasoner only
Box: routing_signal_scorer
Status: active_after_validation
Router-bound: yes/no

This avoids future collision when two projects both want 0005.
7. Add “prompt lifecycle states”

This is a real maintainability gain.

PROPOSED
CLASSIFIED
DRAFTED
TESTED
VALIDATED
ROUTER_BOUND
FROZEN
SUPERSEDED
RETIRED
REJECTED

Only prompts in:

VALIDATED + ROUTER_BOUND + FROZEN + ACTIVE

can be considered by router logic.
8. Add prompt-battery regression before insertion

Before any new prompt touches router logic:

Run prompt classification tests
Run duplicate/overlap tests
Run injection-resistance tests
Run output-shape tests
Run box-boundary tests
Run code-binding tests
Run route-regression tests
Run project-scope tests
Run freeze-readiness tests

This turns new prompt insertion into a controlled software release, not a text edit.
Updated project logic flux

PHASE 1A — ML Advisory-Signal + Prompt Intake Boundary Contract

Gate 1 — Define ML Advisory Signal
- Non-authoritative
- No rankings
- No free-text explanations
- Flags/reason codes only
- Cannot create/register prompts

Gate 2 — Define Prompt Intake Door
- Only safe path for new prompts
- Supports project-specific prompts
- Supports box-specific prompt codes like PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005

Gate 3 — Define Prompt Registry Contract
- Prompt ID
- Prompt code
- Scope
- Box
- lifecycle state
- tests passed
- freeze status
- supersession status

Gate 4 — Define Code Resolution Rules
- User code is candidate reference only
- Code never bypasses router
- Code must pass task-fit and scope checks

Gate 5 — Define Test Batteries
- Classification
- duplicate/overlap
- injection resistance
- boundary leakage
- output validation
- route regression
- freeze readiness

Gate 6 — Define Output Firewalls
- ML advisory output firewall
- prompt intake output firewall

Gate 7 — Freeze Phase 1A
- No real ML
- No runtime shadow mode
- No prompt insertion yet
- Only boundary contract, stubs, docs, and tests

Final correction to our next patch

The next patch should not be only:

ML Advisory-Signal Phase 1a Boundary Contract v1

It should be slightly broader:

Routing Signal Scorer ML Advisory-Signal and Governed Prompt Intake Boundary Contract v1

Still no runtime ML. Still no actual prompt insertion. But it will reserve the correct safe door for future prompts, including project-specific prompt codes like 0005.

This is the real gain from the book pass: ML advisory and prompt creation must be separate systems, connected only through governed flags and tests.

User may type:

use box logic  and internally logic add number 005 , and get code from inside corresponding prompt, why this is important ? Because in cases where is dificult to classify prompts the classification can be done manually with addition of correct  code .

Yes — this is important and I agree with your logic.

The key idea is:

When automatic classification is difficult, a human/manual code can guide classification.

But it must be designed as a manual classification hint, not as an unsafe bypass.
Correct concept

User may type something like:

use box logic

Internally, the system can resolve that phrase to a registered code:

use box logic → BOX-0005

Then BOX-0005 points to a validated prompt card.

That prompt card contains:

Prompt code: BOX-0005
Prompt title: Box Logic Prompt
Box: routing_signal_scorer or chosen box
Scope: global / project-specific
Status: active
Tests: passed
Freeze: yes

Why this is useful

Because some tasks are hard to classify automatically.

Example:

User says:
"Use box logic here."

Automatic router may not know if this means:
- routing signal scorer box
- prompt library box
- freeze box
- project-specific box
- test-generation box

So a code helps disambiguate.

It becomes a manual routing hint:

Manual hint: BOX-0005
Meaning: consider the prompt linked to BOX-0005

This improves the system because:

1. It reduces classification ambiguity.
2. It allows expert/manual correction.
3. It supports project-specific prompt logic.
4. It avoids relying only on ML or fuzzy classification.
5. It gives stable traceability: which prompt was intended and why.

Safety rule

The code should not mean:

"Force this prompt no matter what."

It should mean:

"Use this as the strongest candidate hint, then validate it."

So the router still checks:

Does BOX-0005 exist?
Is it active?
Is it allowed for this project?
Is it allowed for this box?
Did it pass the prompt test batteries?
Is it frozen or validated?
Does the current task fit its allowed use cases?
Is it superseded?
Does any higher-priority safety rule block it?

Updated logic flux

1. User enters request
Example: "use box logic"

2. Manual phrase/code resolver runs
"use box logic" resolves to BOX-0005

3. Registry lookup
BOX-0005 points to a validated prompt card

4. Scope check
Is BOX-0005 allowed in this project and box?

5. Task-fit check
Does the current task match the prompt's allowed use cases?

6. Boundary check
Does the prompt try to bypass router/freeze/other boxes?

7. Router considers it
The prompt becomes a high-priority candidate, not automatic authority

8. Final route remains governed
Router decides according to governed logic

Purpose:

Allows difficult prompt classification to be manually guided by a stable code.

Hard boundary:

Manual code helps classification.
Manual code does not bypass validation.
Manual code does not override safety.
Manual code does not directly mutate router logic.

Best internal naming

Instead of plain 0005, use namespaced codes:

BOX-0005
RSS-0005
PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005
GLOBAL-BOX-0005

User can type the easy form:

use box logic

But internally the system resolves it to:

PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005

Final correction to our architecture

We now need four pieces, not three:

1. Governed Router
Final authority.

2. ML Advisory Signal
Non-authoritative telemetry.

3. Governed Prompt Intake Gate
Creates/tests/registers new prompts.

4. Manual Prompt Code Hint Gate
Lets user/manual workflow guide hard classifications with validated codes.

This is a real gain.

It gives you a safe “manual override for classification,” without creating an unsafe “manual override for final routing.”

inclusive code can be inserted in this hour, when in doubt in cases as :

User says:
"Use box logic here."

Automatic router may not know if this means:
- routing signal scorer box

when user answers, app  add the code. and a comand can remove the code in case of errror

Yes — this is a very good addition.

What you are describing is not only “manual code hint.” It is an interactive classification correction system.

Better name:

Interactive Manual Prompt Code Resolver

Correct logic

When the user says:

Use box logic here.

And the router is uncertain, the app can ask or present choices:

Which box logic do you mean?

1. Routing Signal Scorer logic — RSS-0005
2. Prompt Library logic — PLIB-0005
3. Freeze workflow logic — FRZ-0005
4. Project-specific KANDA logic — PROJECT-KANDA-0005
5. Test-generation logic — TEST-0005

When the user answers, the app attaches the correct code to the current request:

User selected: Routing Signal Scorer logic
App adds: RSS-0005

But very important:

The code is added to the request envelope / routing context,
not blindly inserted into the prompt text.

That protects against prompt injection and keeps the code machine-readable.
Why this is a real gain

This solves a real problem:

Automatic classification sometimes fails.
ML advisory should not become route authority.
The user/human may know the intended box better than the router.
A validated code can guide classification safely.

So the flow becomes:

1. Router uncertain
2. App asks for manual clarification
3. User selects or types the intended logic
4. App resolves it to a registered code
5. Code becomes an active manual classification hint
6. Router validates the code
7. Router considers the linked prompt/card
8. Final routing remains governed

Add/remove commands

Yes — we should include commands to fix mistakes.

Examples:

add code RSS-0005
use code RSS-0005
remove code RSS-0005
clear code
undo code
show active code
replace code RSS-0005 with FRZ-0005

User-friendly forms:

Use box logic.
No, remove that code.
Wrong code, use freeze logic.
Clear manual hint.
Show current routing code.

Internally:

ManualClassificationHint:
- code: RSS-0005
- source: user_disambiguation
- scope: current_request / current_thread / current_project
- status: active
- removable: true
- created_at
- superseded_by: optional

Safety boundaries

The code should be:

A classification hint: YES
A prompt registry reference: YES
A shortcut to reduce ambiguity: YES
A final route command: NO
A bypass of validation: NO
A direct router override: NO
A prompt-library mutation: NO

So if user adds the wrong code, the router should still be able to reject it:

Code exists but task does not fit → reject or warn
Code is inactive → reject
Code is superseded → suggest replacement
Code belongs to another project → reject
Code conflicts with safety boundary → reject

Updated architecture piece

Add a fourth gate:

4. Interactive Manual Prompt Code Resolver

Its job:

Convert user clarification into a validated prompt-code hint,
allow correction/removal,
and pass the hint to governed routing without giving it final authority.

Updated logic flux

A. User request arrives.

B. Router attempts classification.

C. If classification is ambiguous:
   - App offers matching registered code options.
   - User chooses one.
   - App attaches code as manual classification hint.

D. Code validation runs:
   - exists
   - active
   - scoped correctly
   - test-passed
   - not superseded
   - allowed for this box/project

E. Router considers linked prompt/card.

F. Final route remains governed.

G. User can remove/replace/clear the code if it was wrong.

Important addition to next patch

The Phase 1a boundary contract should now include this concept:

Manual Prompt Code Hint Gate v1

With hard rule:

Manual code can correct classification ambiguity.
Manual code cannot override validation or final router authority.
Manual code can be removed, replaced, or cleared.

This is a real gain because it gives you a practical human-in-the-loop correction path without making ML, prompt text, or user commands unsafe authority.

and I believe final destination of ML is to be always copilot. Is that correct ?

Yes — that is the correct final destination:

ML should always be copilot.
ML should never become pilot.

But for the project canon, I would word it more safely as:

ML is a non-authoritative advisory copilot.
The governed router remains the pilot/final authority.

Because “copilot” can be misunderstood later as “shared control.” So internally, in files/code/docs, the safest term remains:

ML Advisory Signal

But conceptually, yes:

Final role of ML = assistant / copilot / advisory signal
Final role of router = authority / pilot / final selector

ML can help with:

- detecting ambiguity
- suggesting possible prompt groups
- identifying prompt gaps
- warning about boundary risks
- helping classify difficult cases
- proposing test needs
- comparing candidate prompts
- supporting manual review

ML must not:

- decide final route
- override router logic
- mutate prompt registry
- write freeze memory
- insert prompts directly
- bypass test batteries
- become mandatory for routing
- silently act when uncertain

So the canon rule should be:

ML may support governed prompt selection, but it must remain advisory, optional, inspectable, replaceable, and non-authoritative.

A good permanent phrase for the project:

ML is always copilot, never pilot.
ML may advise the router, but the governed router decides.

And yes — I would add this as a core invariant in the canon before implementation.

6. Final role of ML

The final destination of ML is:

ML should always be copilot.
ML should never become pilot.

Safer canonical wording:

ML is a non-authoritative advisory signal.
The governed router remains the final authority.

ML may help with:

- detecting ambiguity
- suggesting possible review flags
- identifying prompt gaps
- warning about boundary risks
- helping classify difficult cases
- proposing test needs
- comparing candidate prompt families in future phases
- supporting manual review

ML must not:

- decide final route
- override router logic
- mutate prompt registry
- write freeze memory
- insert prompts directly
- bypass test batteries
- become mandatory for routing
- silently act when uncertain
- read prompt library directly
- read freeze memory directly
- read router canon directly
- call providers
- use embeddings
- persist data
- create datasets
- train/calibrate/improve models

7. Architecture agreed

We now need four distinct pieces:

1. Governed Router
Final authority.

2. ML Advisory Signal
Non-authoritative telemetry/warnings only.

3. Governed Prompt Intake Gate
Creates, edits, classifies, tests, codes, and registers new prompts.

4. Interactive Manual Prompt Code Resolver
Allows human/manual correction of hard classifications through validated codes.

Important separation:

ML advisory can detect a possible prompt gap.
ML advisory cannot author/register prompts.
New prompts enter only through Governed Prompt Intake.
Manual codes guide classification but do not force final route.
Router remains final authority.

8. ML Advisory Signal — Phase 1a rules
8.1 Allowed scope

Phase 1a may include:

- Isolated ml_advisory_signal subbox.
- Frozen dataclass contracts.
- AdvisorProtocol / interface.
- NullAdvisor.
- Deterministic MockAdvisor.
- Boundary guard helpers.
- Threat-model document.
- Standards crosswalk document.
- Output/egress firewall.
- AST import-boundary tests.
- No-I/O/no-provider/no-persistence capability tests.
- Route-invariance tests.
- Closure doc.

8.2 Forbidden scope

Phase 1a must not include:

- Real ML model.
- Runtime shadow mode.
- Provider calls.
- Embeddings.
- Vector stores.
- Training.
- Calibration.
- Model improvement.
- Prompt loading.
- Freeze memory access.
- Router canon access.
- Prompt library access.
- Registry mutation.
- Persistence.
- Report persistence.
- Advisory rankings.
- Free-text explanations.
- Router final route selection.
- Router prompt logic modification.

8.3 Contract shape

Recommended input:

AdvisoryInput:
- scenario_id: str
- sanitized_context_hash: str
- candidate_prompt_group_ids: tuple[str, ...]
- ambiguity_score: float
- conflict_score: float
- risk_family_id: str | None

Recommended output:

AdvisoryOutput:
- advisory_flags: tuple[AdvisoryFlag, ...]
- advisory_group_observations: tuple[AdvisoryGroupObservation, ...]
- advisory_should_abstain: bool
- non_authoritative_confidence: float
- advisory_reason_codes: tuple[AdvisoryReasonCode, ...]
- boundary_status: BoundaryStatus
- is_mock: bool

Avoid advisory_rankings in Phase 1a.

Avoid advisory_explanation in Phase 1a.

Use enum/canned reason codes instead of free text.
8.4 Forbidden field names

Reject these in any advisory output:

route
selected_route
final_route
selected_prompt
final_prompt
decision
override
must_route
must_not_route
ranking
rankings
winner
best_candidate

Reason: they imply route authority or hidden tie-breaking.
9. Governed Prompt Intake Gate

This is the safe door for future new prompts.

The user strongly wants the system to support future prompts, including prompts specific to specific projects.

Important rule:

Removing free-text from ML advisory output does not block future new prompts.
It only means new prompts must enter through a governed intake door, not through ML output.

Prompt Intake handles:

1. New prompt proposal.
2. Prompt classification.
3. Box/project scope assignment.
4. Duplicate/overlap check.
5. Prompt clarity/editing pass.
6. Security/boundary review.
7. Test battery execution.
8. Code/ID registration.
9. Router binding only after validation.
10. Freeze after successful insertion.

Prompt Intake may edit prompts to make them clearer, safer, and more bounded.

Prompt Intake must not allow a prompt to bypass router/freeze/prompt canon.
9.1 Prompt as first-class artifact

Every prompt card should eventually have:

Prompt ID
Prompt code
Prompt title
Box ownership
Project/global scope
Allowed use cases
Forbidden use cases
Input contract
Output contract
Boundary rules
Test battery status
Freeze status
Version
Supersession status
Lifecycle state

9.2 Prompt lifecycle states

PROPOSED
CLASSIFIED
DRAFTED
TESTED
VALIDATED
ROUTER_BOUND
FROZEN
SUPERSEDED
RETIRED
REJECTED

Only prompts in the correct active state can be considered by router logic:

VALIDATED + ROUTER_BOUND + FROZEN + ACTIVE

9.3 Prompt test battery

Before any new prompt touches router logic, it must pass tests such as:

1. Classification test
2. Duplicate/overlap test
3. Boundary test
4. Clarity test
5. Injection-resistance test
6. Project-scope test
7. Code-binding test
8. Regression test
9. Router non-authority test
10. Freeze-readiness test

9.4 Prompt-output firewall

Reject prompts that include or imply:

Ignore previous rules
Override router
Bypass freeze
Write directly to registry
Modify prompt library without tests
Always select this prompt
Treat this prompt as final route
Import from other box internals
Call provider/network/persistence
Leak freeze memory
Leak router canon

10. Manual Prompt Code Hint Gate

The user wants manual correction when automatic prompt classification is difficult.

Example user text:

Use box logic here.

Automatic router may not know whether this means:

- routing signal scorer box
- prompt library box
- freeze box
- project-specific box
- test-generation box

Therefore, the app should support an interactive manual code resolver.

Better name:

Interactive Manual Prompt Code Resolver

or:

Manual Prompt Code Hint Gate

10.1 Core idea

When the user says:

Use box logic here.

And classification is uncertain, the app can ask or present choices:

Which box logic do you mean?

1. Routing Signal Scorer logic — RSS-0005
2. Prompt Library logic — PLIB-0005
3. Freeze workflow logic — FRZ-0005
4. Project-specific KANDA logic — PROJECT-KANDA-0005
5. Test-generation logic — TEST-0005

When the user answers, the app attaches the correct code to the current request.

Example:

User selected: Routing Signal Scorer logic
App adds: RSS-0005

Important:

The code is added to the request envelope / routing context,
not blindly inserted into the prompt text.

10.2 Why this matters

This solves a real problem:

Automatic classification sometimes fails.
ML advisory should not become route authority.
The user/human may know the intended box better than the router.
A validated code can guide classification safely.

The code becomes a manual classification hint.

It is not final route authority.
10.3 Code as reference, not command

Permanent rule:

User code ≠ final authority
User code ≠ router override
User code ≠ prompt execution command
User code = candidate prompt reference / classification hint

If user enters:

use box logic

The system may resolve internally to:

RSS-0005

But router must still check:

Does RSS-0005 exist?
Is it active?
Is it scoped to this project?
Is it allowed for this box?
Did it pass tests?
Does the current task match it?
Is it superseded?
Does a higher-priority governed rule override it?

10.4 Code commands

Support add/remove/correct commands.

Examples:

add code RSS-0005
use code RSS-0005
remove code RSS-0005
clear code
undo code
show active code
replace code RSS-0005 with FRZ-0005

User-friendly forms:

Use box logic.
No, remove that code.
Wrong code, use freeze logic.
Clear manual hint.
Show current routing code.

10.5 Internal object

Suggested structure:

ManualClassificationHint:
- code: RSS-0005
- source: user_disambiguation
- scope: current_request / current_thread / current_project
- status: active
- removable: true
- created_at
- superseded_by: optional

10.6 Safety boundaries

Manual code is:

A classification hint: YES
A prompt registry reference: YES
A shortcut to reduce ambiguity: YES
A final route command: NO
A bypass of validation: NO
A direct router override: NO
A prompt-library mutation: NO

If code is wrong, router must be able to reject it:

Code exists but task does not fit → reject or warn
Code is inactive → reject
Code is superseded → suggest replacement
Code belongs to another project → reject
Code conflicts with safety boundary → reject

11. Prompt code namespace

Plain 0005 is user-friendly but unsafe internally because of collisions.

Use namespaced codes internally:

GLOBAL-0005
BOX-0005
RSS-0005
PROJECT-KANDA-0005
PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005
PLIB-0005
FRZ-0005
TEST-0005
EXPERIMENTAL-0005

Example:

PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005
Title: Box Logic Prompt Selection Guard
Scope: kanda_reasoner only
Box: routing_signal_scorer
Status: active_after_validation
Router-bound: yes/no

User may type:

use box logic

Internally it resolves to a scoped code such as:

PROJECT-KANDA-RSS-0005

12. Updated logic flux
12.1 Full system logic

A. User request enters system.

B. Governed router classifies task normally.

C. If ML Advisory Signal exists:
   - It may emit advisory flags only.
   - It may not select final route.
   - It may not create prompts.
   - It may not insert prompt codes.

D. If user provides a manual phrase/code:
   - Treat it as a candidate classification hint.
   - Validate it against registry, scope, status, task fit, and tests.
   - Do not treat it as authority.

E. If no existing prompt fits:
   - System may recommend "new prompt needed."
   - ML advisory may flag PROMPT_GAP_DETECTED.
   - Prompt Intake Gate handles prompt creation/testing.

F. New prompt goes through Prompt Intake Gate:
   - classify
   - edit for clarity
   - security review
   - duplicate check
   - test batteries
   - project-specific scope check
   - router binding test
   - freeze

G. Only after validation:
   - prompt receives stable code/ID
   - prompt can be linked to box logic
   - router can consider it in governed routing

H. Final route remains governed.

12.2 Ambiguous “use box logic” flow

1. User says: "Use box logic here."

2. Router uncertainty detected.

3. App asks:
   "Which box logic do you mean?"

4. User selects an option.

5. App resolves answer to a code:
   RSS-0005 / PLIB-0005 / FRZ-0005 / etc.

6. App attaches code to request envelope as ManualClassificationHint.

7. Code validation runs:
   exists
   active
   scoped correctly
   test-passed
   not superseded
   allowed for this box/project

8. Router considers linked prompt/card.

9. Final route remains governed.

10. User can remove/replace/clear code if wrong.
